Elementary equivalences for blocks with normal elementary abelian defect group of rank 2
Mary Schaps, Zehavit Zvi

Abstract
We consider the effect of performing an elementary equivalence as defined by Okuyama on a group block of form F ⁡ ( C p × C p ) ⋊ C r F(C_{p}\times C_{p})\rtimes C_{r} , for a field F F of characteristic p p . If I = { 0 , 1 , 2 , … ​ r − 1 } I=\{0,1,2,\dots r-1\} is the set of residues corresponding to the simple modules of F ​ C r FC_{r} , the elementary equivalence is determined by a proper, non-empty subset I 0 ⊂ I I_{0}\subset I , and the corresponding elementary tilting complex is completely determined by I 0 I_{0} . We give a catalog of homogeneous maps between irreducible components of the elementary tilting complex. When the subset I 0 I_{0} is an interval, we prove that the maps in the catalog are sufficient to describe all homogeneous maps between two irreducible components.



1
INTRODUCTION

This work concerns blocks over a field
F
of odd finite characteristic
p
, with elementary abelian defect group
D=C_{p}\times C_{p}
. We restrict to odd characteristic because the case of
C_{2}\times C_{2}
has been intensively studied. The defect group
C_{p}\times C_{p}
is the defect group with the next largest rank after the well-studied case of cyclic defect group.

We are interested in the tilting theory
of these blocks, trying to apply some of the insights gathered from the cyclic defect case
[
R1
,
SZ2
,
SZ
]
.
In that case, every block can be obtained from a block with normal defect group by a sequence of elementary tiltings known as mutations. Okuyama
[
O
]
found some important group blocks as the result of a sequence of more general elementary equivalences in the case
p=3
. The Broué conjecture for principal blocks when
p=3
was eventually proven by Koshitani and Kunugi in
[
KK
]
but is still open for larger primes. Unfortunately, one of Okuyama’s central lemmas is less useful for primes
p\geq 5
, so his method had not found ready application to other primes.

A study of the blocks of subgroups of the groups in the Atlas of Simple Groups
[
C
]
shows that most of the abelian defect groups are either cyclic or of the form
D=C_{p}\times C_{p}
, and all have a property we will define later as being balanced. Furthermore, the Brauer correspondents of these blocks are usually of the form
D\rtimes C_{r}
or
D\rtimes D_{n}
. In this work, we are taking up the first case,
D\rtimes C_{r}
and asking what sort of algebras can be reached by a single elementary equivalence, as a function of the prime
p
and the number
r
of simple modules in the original block of cyclic defect.

2
DEFINITIONS AND NOTATION

2.1
Blocks with normal defect groups

The blocks with normal defect groups were studied in depth by several authors
[
Lan
]
,
[
RR
]
,
[
Sw
]
and also
[
K
]
. We will review that part of the theory which we need. By Brauer’s First Main Theorem, we know that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the blocks
B
of
FG
with defect group
D
, and the blocks
b
of
FN_{G}(D)
with defect group
D

[
CR
]
. In order to investigate the relationship between
B
and
b
we must know more about the structure of the blocks of
FN_{G}(D)
. We restricted ourselves to Brauer correspondents which are Morita equivalent as blocks to
F[(C_{p}\times C_{p})\rtimes H]
, with
H=C_{r}
for
r
prime to
p
. We will show that we can choose a basis for the radical of
F[D]
which are eigenvectors for the generator of
C_{r}
and we will assume that the eigenvalues of the generators of this radical are inverse to each other.

2.2
The quiver of a block

Let
A
be a
K
-algebra, where
K
is an algebraically closed field with
A/\operatorname{Rad}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus M_{n_{i}}(K)
. Let
f_{1},\dots,f_{t}
be a set of primitive idempotents, one from each matrix block, and set

n^{\prime}_{ij}=\dim(f_{i}(J/J^{2})f_{j})

for
J=\operatorname{Rad}(A)
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
quiver
of
A
is the directed graph with
t
vertices, where
t
is the number of simple modules, and
n^{\prime}_{ij}
arrows from
j
to
i
. Then
n^{\prime}_{ij}
is the number of irreducible morphisms from
j
to
i
.

In
[
S
]
, results were quoted showing that:

1.

The block idempotents of
FH
embed in
FG
.

2.

\operatorname{Rad}(FG)
is generated by
J=\operatorname{Rad}(FD)
.

3.

The action of
H
on
D
induces an action of
H
on
J/J^{2}
with character
\chi
, and there is an
H
-submodule
N
of
J
isomorphic to
J/J^{2}
.

4.

The quiver of
G
is the McKay graph
D(H,\chi)
for the character
\chi
of
H
.

Gerstenhaber and Schaps go on to show in
[
GS
]
that the
H
-module
J/J^{2}
can be embedded as a submodule of
J
of dimension
d
over
F
. If
H
is abelian, it decomposes as a direct sum of one-dimensional submodules, so that we can find a basis for
J
of idempotents for the action of
H
.

2.3
The McKay graph

In
[
M
]
, McKay introduced a graph that he called the representation graph and which later was called the McKay graph by Schaps, Shapira and Shlomo in
[
SSS
]
.

Let
H
be a finite group, and let
F
be a field of characteristic zero or of positive characteristic
p
,
p\nmid\left|H\right|
. Let
K
be an extension of
F
which is a splitting field for
H
. Let the character table of
H
be considered as having entries in
K
. Let
\chi
be a character of
H
, with kernel
N
. If
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{t}
is the complete set of irreducible characters of
H
, and
\chi\cdot\theta_{j}
is defined by pointwise multiplication of the values for the various conjugacy classes, define the natural number
n_{ij}
as the multiplicity of the constituent
\theta_{i}
in the product character
\chi\cdot\theta_{j}
. Since the
\theta_{i}
form an orthonormal basis, with respect to an inner product ( -, -), this is given by
n_{ij}=(\theta_{i},\chi\cdot\theta_{j}
).

Definition 2.2
.

If
H
is a finite group,
K
is a sufficiently large field of characteristic prime to
\left|H\right|
, and
\chi
is a
K
character of
H
, the
McKay graph

D
(
H
,
\chi
) is the directed graph with

(i)

t
vertices labelled by the irreducible characters
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{t}
of
H
.

(ii)

For each pair
(\theta_{i}
,
\theta_{j})
of characters,
n_{ij}
arrows from
\theta_{j}
to
\theta_{i}
, where

n_{ij}=(\theta_{i},\chi\cdot\theta_{j}).

In (
[
SSS
]
, §3), it was shown that for a graph algebra of the form
(C_{p}^{d})\rtimes H
, where
p\nmid\left|H\right|
, the quiver was given by the McKay graph, and a method was also given in
[
SSS
]
for writing down the relations on the Morita equivalent basic algebra.

2.4
The group algebra
F[(C_{p}\times C_{p})\rtimes C_{r}]

We now restrict to the case most common for non-cyclic defect, the case of
d=2
and
H=C_{r}
, with
r
not divisible by
p
. By the results we quoted from
[
GS
]
, we can find two generators
x
and
y
for the radical
J
which are eigenvectors for the action of
C_{r}
.
There are many ways to choose a pair of eigenvalues of the action of
C_{r}
, but what usually occurs in the examples is that there is an automorphism that interchanges
x
with
y
, and the generator of the
C_{r}
with its inverse, which means that the idempotent of
y
is the inverse of the idempotent of
x
, so that the action of
C_{r}
on
xy
is trivial.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
G=(C_{p}\times C_{p})\rtimes C_{r}
, and let
x
and
y
be generators of
J
which are eigenvectors for the action of
C_{r}
. We will say that the action is
balanced
if the eigenvalue of the action of
C_{r}
on
x
is the inverse of the eigenvalue of
y
.

Example 1
.

If
H
is
C_{4}=\langle a\rangle
,
G=(C_{5}\times C_{5})\rtimes C_{4}
, where the generator
a
of
C_{4}
acts on the generators
c
and
d
of
C_{5}\times C_{5}
by sending
c
to
c^{2}
and
d
to
d^{3}
. Let
i\in F
be a primitive 4th root of unity, with irreducible characters
\theta_{j}(a^{k})=i^{(j-1)k}
, where
j=1,\dots,4
and
k=0,\dots,3
. Let
e_{u}
,
u=1,\dots,4
, be the idempotents of
KC_{4}
. Set
\chi=\theta_{2}+\theta_{4}
.
Then,

\chi\cdot\theta_{j}=\theta_{j+1}+\theta_{j-1}

where the indices are taken mod 4. Since
2
and
3
are primitive fourth roots of unity modulo
5
,
we can choose the generators as follows

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=c+3c^{2}+2c^{3}+4c^{4}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=d+2d^{3}+3d^{2}+4d^{4}.

Then
a^{-1}xa=c^{2}+3c^{4}+2c+4c^{3}=2x
and

a^{-1}ya=d^{3}+2d^{4}+3d+4d^{2}=3y

The quiver of
FG
is given by

\textstyle{{{}^{1}\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bullet^{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{}_{0}\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\bullet_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with the relations:

\displaystyle x_{u(u+1)}

\displaystyle=e_{u}xe_{u+1}

\displaystyle y_{u(u-1)}

\displaystyle=e_{u}ye_{u-1}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=x_{01}+x_{12}+x_{23}+x_{30}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=y_{10}+x_{03}+x_{32}+x_{21}

\displaystyle xy

\displaystyle=yx

\displaystyle x^{5}

\displaystyle=0,y^{5}=0

2.5
The structure of the projective indecomposable modules
P_{t}
for
F[(C_{p}\times C_{p})\rtimes C_{r}]

Since
C_{r}
is abelian, the simple modules of
FG
, which correspond to the simple modules of
FC_{r}
, are one-dimensional. They bijectively correspond to the idempotents of
FC_{r}
. Using the fact that powers of
\xi
and
a
can be taken modulo
r
, we recall that the central idempotents of
FC_{r}
are given by

\displaystyle e_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\xi^{-it}a^{i},t=0,\dots,r-1.

We consider one particular idempotent
e_{t}
and its projective module
P_{t}
. We first consider the vector space
S_{t}=\langle e_{t}\rangle
, and show that it is a one-dimensional
C_{r}
-module under multiplication in
FC_{r}
.

\displaystyle a\cdot e_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\xi^{-it}a^{i+1}

\displaystyle=\xi^{t}\cdot\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\xi^{-(i+1)t}a^{i+1}

\displaystyle=\xi^{t}e_{t}.

By §2.3, for
A=FG
, the projective modules are
P_{t}=FGe_{t}
. If
J=\operatorname{Rad}(FD)
and
I=\operatorname{Rad}(FG)=JFG
then
\operatorname{Rad}(P_{t})=I\cap P_{t}
and
S_{t}=P_{t}/\operatorname{Rad}(P_{t})
is the simple module corresponding to
e_{t}
.

We have assumed that the characteristic of
F
is
p
. Since
D\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{p}\times C_{p}
,
FD=F[x,y]/(x^{p},y^{p})
. Since
C_{r}
is an abelian group and
p\nmid r
we set
FC_{r}=\oplus Fe_{t}
. The structure of these blocks is treated in
[
Lan
]
,
[
Sw
]
,
[
K
]
,
[
RR
]
. Our notation follows that of
[
GS
]
in the simpler case where there is no inertial subgroup. We know that the dimension of
FD
is
p^{2}
and there is a basis
x^{i}y^{j}
. The module can be represented as a diamond of length
p
on each side.

2.6
Derived equivalence

We fix an abelian category
\mathcal{C}
and we denote by
Ch(\mathcal{C})

the category of cochain complexes of objects of
\mathcal{C}
.
The differentials of the complex are
morphisms
\left\{d_{n}:C_{n}\rightarrow C_{n+1}\right\}

satisfying
d_{n+1}\circ d_{n}=0
. For any given complex
C^{\bullet}
, the
cocycles
Z^{n}(C^{\bullet})
, coboundaries
B^{n}(C^{\bullet})
, and cohomology modules

H^{n}(C^{\bullet})=Z^{n}(C^{\bullet})/B^{n}(C^{\bullet})
are defined as in such standard texts as
[
W
]
.

Definition 2.4
.

A morphism
f_{\bullet}:C^{\bullet}\rightarrow D^{\bullet}
of cochain complexes is called
a
quasi-isomorphism
if the induced maps
H^{n}\left(C^{\bullet}\right)\rightarrow H^{n}(D^{\bullet})

are all isomorphisms.

Definition 2.5
.

A cochain complex
C

is called
bounded

if almost all the
C^{n}
are zero. The

complex
C

is
bounded
below

if there is a bound
a

such that
C^{n}=0

for all
n<a
. The cochain complexes which are partially or fully bounded
form full subcategories
Ch^{b},\thinspace Ch^{+},\thinspace Ch^{-}
of

Ch(\mathcal{C})
.

Definition 2.6
.

The
derived category
of an abelian category
\mathcal{C}
is the category obtained from
Ch(\mathcal{C})
by adding
formal inverses to all the quasi-isomorphisms between chain complexes. It is called the
bounded derived category
and denoted
D^{b}(\mathcal{C})
if we consider only bounded complexes.
A
derived equivalence
between two abelian categories is an equivalence of categories between their derived categories.

Definition 2.7
.

We say that a chain map
f:C\rightarrow D
is
null
homotopic
if there are maps
s_{n}:C^{n}\rightarrow D^{n+1}
such
that
f=ds+sd
.

Definition 2.8
.

Two chain maps
f,g:C\rightarrow D
are
chain homotopic

if their difference
f-g
is null homotopic, in other words, if
f-g=sd+ds

for some
s
. The maps
\left\{s_{n}\right\}
are called a
homotopy

from
f
to
g
. We say that
f:C\rightarrow D
is a

homotopy equivalence
if there is a map
g:D\rightarrow C

such that
g\circ f
is chain homotopic to the identity map
id_{C}

and
f\circ g
is chain homotopic to the identity map
id_{D}
.

2.7
Tilting complexes

We now consider complexes
T
of projective left modules over an associative ring
R
. The
notation
T[n]
denotes the complex that is isomorphic to
T
as
a module but in which the gradation has been shifted
n
places to
the left and the differential is the shift of the differential multiplied
by
(-1)^{n}
.

For any ring
R
, let
D^{b}(R)
be the derived category
of bounded complexes of
R
-modules.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
R
be a Noetherian ring. A bounded complex

T
of finitely generated projective
R
-modules is called a
tilting
complex
if:

(i)

Hom
{}_{D^{b}\left(R\right)}\left(T,T[n]\right)=0

whenever

n\neq 0
.

(ii)

For any indecomposable projective

P
, define
the stalk complex to be the complex

P^{.}:0\rightarrow P\rightarrow 0
.

Then every such

P^{.}

is in the triangulated category
generated by the direct summands of direct sums of copies of

T.

A complex
T
satisfying only (i) is called
a
partial tilting complex.

Definition 2.10
.

Fix an abelian category
\mathcal{C}
and the
category of cochain complexes
Ch(\mathcal{C})
. For two complexes

X

and
Y

denote by
Z(X,Y)
the set of morphisms
from
X

to
Y

which are homotopic to zero. The
collection of all
Z(X,Y)

forms a subgroup of Hom
{}_{C(\mathcal{C})}(X,Y)
.
Denote by
K(\mathcal{C})

the

quotient category,
i.e.

K(\mathcal{C})
is the category having the same objects
as
Ch(\mathcal{C})
but with morphisms

Hom
{{}_{K}}_{(\mathcal{C}}{{}_{)}(X,Y)=}
Hom
Ch
(C
{{}_{)}(X,Y)/Z(X,Y)}
,

so that two homotopic maps are identified.
The quotient category
K(\mathcal{C})

is called the
homotopy
category
, and a homotopy equivalence between
complexes is an isomorphism in the homotopy category. For an abelian category
{\mathcal{C},}

K^{-}(\mathcal{C})

is the homotopy category of right bounded complexes in
{\mathcal{C}}
,
and similarly one can define
K^{+}(\mathcal{C})
.

The derived category is not an abelian category, but it is a triangulated category.
The original theory of tilting concerned modules called tilting modules.
Happel, in
[
H
]
showed that if there was a tilting between two algebras
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
,
it induced a functor which was an equivalence of their derived bounded categories.
Rickard
[
R1
]
then proved a converse when tilting modules were replaced by tilting complexes,
namely, that there is a tilting complex
T
over
\Lambda
with endomorphism
ring End
{}_{D^{b}\left(\Lambda\right)}(T)^{op}\cong\Gamma
.

Let

\Lambda-Proj
be the abelian category of projective
\Lambda
-modules.
Let
Sum-T
be the additive category of all the direct sums of copies
of
T
(as a full subcategory of
K^{-}(\Lambda-Proj))
.
From Lemma 3.3.1 of
[
KZ2
]
we have that
K^{-}(\Gamma-Proj)

is equivalent to
K^{-}\left(Sum-T\right)
as triangulated categories.
Thus, every tilting complex can be transformed into a projective complex,
and to calculate the required endomorphism ring of the
tilting complex, it will be sufficient to find chain maps between the
components of the tilting complex which are not homotopic to zero.

2.8
Elementary equivalences

Of particular importance in this work are the elementary equivalences, which go back to the work of Rickard
[
R2
]
and
Okuyama
[
O
]
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
A
be a finite-dimensional basic algebra, with projective modules
P_{j},j\in I
.

Fix a subset
I_{0}\subset I
.
For any
i\in I
we define a complex by

T_{i}=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{cccc}(0th)&&(1st)&\\
P_{i}&\longrightarrow&0&\>\>i\in I_{0}\\
Q_{i}&\overset{\pi_{i}}{\longrightarrow}&P_{i}&\>\>i\in I-I_{0}\end{array}&\end{cases}

where
Q_{i}\overset{\pi_{i}}{\longrightarrow}P_{i}
is
a minimal projective presentation of the minimal kernel with quotient having only simples from
I-I_{0}
.
Now we define
T:=\varoplus_{i{\in I}}T_{i}
.
The algebra
elementary eqivalent
of
A
through
T
is is
A^{\prime}\cong(\operatorname{End}_{D^{b}(A)}T)^{op}
.

2.9
Homogeneous maps

To each
i\in I
,
we can associate an idempotent
\tilde{e}_{i}
with
1_{A}
=
\mathrel{{\mathop{\sum}\limits}}
\tilde{e}_{i}
.

Definition 2.12
.

For
i,j\in\{0,\dots,r-1\}
let

P_{i}=Ae_{i}P_{j}=Ae_{j}
.
The map

f:P_{i}\to P_{j}

is
homogeneous
if

f(e_{i})=\epsilon x^{k}y^{\ell}e_{j}

for integers
0\leq k,\ell\leq p-1
,
\epsilon\in F
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let

\displaystyle f:

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}P_{i}\to\bigoplus_{j=1}^{r}P_{k}.

Then
f
will be called
homogeneous
if every component in
\bigoplus_{i,k=1}^{r}Hom(P_{i},P_{k})
is a homogeneous map

f_{ik}:P_{i}\to P_{k}

as in Definition
2.12
.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
l^{\bullet}
be the chain map:

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}P_{\mu_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\{d_{it}\}}
\scriptstyle{\{b_{ij}\}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{t=1}^{k}P_{\kappa_{t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\{f_{tu}\}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}P_{\nu_{j}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\{c_{ju}\}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{u=1}^{\ell}P_{\lambda_{u}}}

We say that
l^{\bullet}
is
homogeneous
if every vertical and horizontal map is homogeneous as in Definition
2.13
, and for every pair
(i,u)
and for all
j
and
t
we get

\displaystyle\sum^{k}_{t=1}f_{tu}\circ d_{it}=\sum^{n}_{j=1}c_{ju}\circ b_{ij}

Definition 2.15
.

A tilting complex
T=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}T_{i}
over
A
is called homogeneous if each component of the differentials is
\pm 1
times a monomial in
x,y
times the appropriate idempotent.

3
MAPS BETWEEN COMPONENTS OF AN ELEMENTARY TILTING COMPLEX

In order to describe the result of acting on the group algebra
A=F[C_{p}\times C_{p}))\rtimes C_{r}]
by an elementary equivalence, we must calculate the endomorphism ring of the tilting complex
T=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}T_{i}
. To simplify all calculations, we will assume that the tilting complex
T
is homogeneous, a condition which can be achieved using Lemma
3.2
below.

Let
I=\{0,1,2\dots,r-1\}
be arranged in a circle in the clockwise direction. Since
I_{0}
is non-empty, removing the points of
I_{0}
separates the circle into a finite number of intervals
J_{1},\dots,J_{m}
which we will call
arcs
. For a particular
J_{i}
, where
1\leq i\leq m
, we let
u_{i}
be the first element of
I_{0}
in the counter-clockwise direction and let
v_{i}
be the first element of
I_{0}
in the clockwise direction. If
I_{0}
contains a single point
w
, we will have
m=1
and
u_{1}=v_{1}=w
.

We denote the
r
-residue of
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by
\bar{n}
. Any
t\in I-{I_{0}}
belongs to an arc
J_{i}=[\overline{u_{i}+1},\dots,\overline{t-1},t,\overline{t+1},\dots,\overline{v_{i}-1}]
with the understanding that
0
follows
r-1
. The arc
J_{i}
is preceded by an interval
I_{0}^{i}=[\overline{v_{i-1}},\overline{v_{i-1}+1},\dots,\overline{u_{i}-1},\overline{u_{i}}]\subseteq I_{0}
and followed by the interval
I_{0}^{i+1}
.

Definition 3.1
.

If
t,\tilde{t}\in I
both lie in one of the intervals
I_{0}^{i}
,

I_{0}^{i+1}
or
J_{i}
, we will write
t\preceq\tilde{t}
to indicate
\tilde{t}
either equals
t
or is subsequent to
t
in the interval.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
t,\tilde{t}\in I
both lie in
J_{i}
and
s
lies in
I_{0}^{i}
, then

\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+\overline{t-s}=\overline{\tilde{t}-s}.

Similarly, if
s\in I_{0}^{i+1}
, then

\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+\overline{s-\tilde{t}}=\overline{s-t}.

Proof.

Using the notation above,
I_{0}^{i}\cup J_{i}
can be written as an ordered interval of length less than or equal to
r
. If
v=v_{i-1}
is the first element of
I^{i}_{0}
, then we renumber
I
by replacing each

k\in I
by
k^{\prime}=\overline{k-v}
. If we identify
s^{\prime},u^{\prime},\tilde{t}^{\prime}
, which are residues, with the corresponding integers between
0
and
r-1
, then we have
s^{\prime}<t^{\prime}\leq\tilde{t}^{\prime}
, so that

t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}=\overline{t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}}
,
\tilde{t}^{\prime}-t^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}^{\prime}-t^{\prime}}
and
\tilde{t}^{\prime}-s^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}^{\prime}-s^{\prime}}
. Since the renumbering is just a cyclic permutation and does not affect the residue of the differences between two residues, and since, as integers,

(\tilde{t}^{\prime}-t^{\prime})+(t^{\prime}-s^{\prime})=\tilde{t}^{\prime}-s^{\prime},

we obtain the desired result.
∎

In this article, we fix one arc
J_{i}
and we determine all the irreducible maps
internal to the sets
I_{0}^{i}\cup J_{i}\cup I_{0}^{i+1}
. If
m=1
, there is just a single arc
J_{1}
, and a single complementary interval
I_{0}
. Then the maps we will construct will generate the entire endomorphism ring. We will leave to a sequel the case where
m>1
. For simplicity of notations, we will write
J=J_{i}
,
u=u_{i},v=v_{i}
.

We first determine the maps between the projective modules in the block of normal defect group.

Lemma 3.2
.

We have a group block
F[(C_{p}\times C_{p})\rtimes C_{r}]
with indecomposible projective modules
P_{0},P_{1},\dots P_{r-1}
. Suppose we are given a homogeneous map
P_{i}\to P_{k}
, for
0\leq i,k\leq r-1
.

1.

If
\overline{k-i}<p
then there are homogeneous maps
f(e_{i})=x^{\overline{k-i}+hr}(xy)^{q}
, where

h
is an integer satisfying
0\leq h\leq\frac{p-1-\overline{k-i}}{r}
and
0\leq q\leq p-1-(\overline{k-i}+hr)
.

If
\overline{i-k}<p
then there are homogeneous maps
f(e_{i})=y^{\overline{i-k}+hr}(xy)^{q}
, where

h
is an integer satisfying
0\leq h\leq\frac{p-1-\overline{i-k}}{r}
and
0\leq q\leq p-1-(\overline{i-k}+hr)
.

2.

The homogeneous maps give a basis for the homomorphisms from
P_{i}\to P_{k}
thus we get

f(e_{i})=1_{\overline{k-i}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{k-i}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q=0}^{\lfloor p-1-(\overline{k-i}+hr)\rfloor}a_{q}^{h}\cdot x^{\overline{k-i}+hr}(xy)^{q}e_{k}

+1_{\overline{i-k}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{i-k}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q=0}^{\lfloor p-1-(\overline{i-k}+hr)\rfloor}b_{q}^{h}\cdot y^{\overline{i-k}+hr}(xy)^{q}e_{k}.

Note that if
r>p
then
h=0
.

Proof.

1.

By standard arguments of representation theory, the image of
e_{i}
in
P_{k}
must be a linear combination of basis elements
e_{i}x^{s}y^{t}e_{k}
. Starting with
e_{k}
, if we multiply by
x
we get a basis element
e_{k-1}xe_{k}
. If we repeat this
s
times we get
e_{k-s}x^{s}e_{k}
. The first time that
e_{k-s}=e_{i}
will be when
s=\overline{k-i}
. This can only occur if
\overline{k-i}<p
; otherwise we get
0
. If we continue multiplying
h
times by
x^{r}
we keep on returning to
e_{i}
as long as
s=\overline{k-i}+hr<p
. Multiplication by
y
is dual, giving the elements
e_{k+t}y^{t}e_{k}
. For the first time that
e_{k+t}=e_{i}
is when
t=\overline{i-k}
and this can only occur if
\overline{i-k}<p
. If we continue multiplying
h
times by
y^{r}
we keep on returning to
e_{i}
as long as
t=\overline{i-k}+hr<p
.

If
e_{i}x^{s}e_{k}\neq 0
then also
e_{i}x^{s}(xy)^{q}e_{k}\neq 0
as long as
s+q\leq p-1
. Dually if
e_{i}y^{t}e_{k}\neq 0
then also
e_{i}y^{t}(xy)^{q}e_{k}\neq 0
as long as
t+q\leq p-1
. Note that if
i=k
there are maps
f(e_{i})=\sum_{q=0}^{p-1}c_{q}(xy)^{q}e_{k}
.

2.

The general homomorphism is a linear combination of the homogeneous maps we found in
1
. We added the two characteristic functions
1_{\overline{k-i}<p},1_{\overline{i-k}<p}
to indicate when part of the sum is zero.

∎

We recall from Definition
2.11
that the irreducible components
T_{i}
are of two different types dependent on
i
. If
i\in I_{0}
, then
T_{i}
is a stalk complex, whereas if
i\in I-I_{0}
, it is a complex with nonzero components in two degrees. We will be working in the homotopy category of complexes of projective modules and we will denote
T_{i}
by
P^{\prime}_{i}
when regarded as an element of the homotopy category. What we will do is propose a catalog of irreducible maps between the various
P^{\prime}_{i}
, and then demonstrate that a general homogenous map from
P^{\prime}_{i}
to
P^{\prime}_{j}
can be written as a composition of maps in the catalog.

3.1
Catalog of intra-arc maps

We now list the irreducible maps from
P^{\prime}_{t}
to
P^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
for
t,t^{\prime}
in the interval

I_{0}^{i}\cup J_{i}\cup I_{0}^{i+1}
. The maps will generally be described by powers of
x
and
y
, but we will also need the following special maps in the catalog:

Definition 3.2
.

Let
t\in J
in cyclic order, then the chain maps
(\xi_{1},x)
,
(\xi_{2},y)
are as follows

•

, as defined at the beginning of

, there is a sequence of maps, whose properties depend on the relative sizes of
r
and
p
and the location of

. The maps we will give are all well-defined and not homotopic to zero.

, maps as in Diagram 2.

Case 3: If
1\leq\mid J\mid<p-1
, maps as in Diagram 3.

\scriptstyle{\binom{x^{j-2}}{y^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{v-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{2}}

\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{P_{v_{i-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{u-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{P_{u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{x^{j}}

\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{y^{j}}
\textstyle{P_{v+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{P_{u_{i+1}}}

We recall that we denoted
T_{i}
in Definition
2.11
by
P^{\prime}_{i}
.
For
i,i+1\in J
we denote by
\alpha_{i,i+1}:P^{\prime}_{i}\to P^{\prime}_{i+1}
the appropriate adjacent map from the diagrams, and by
\alpha_{i+1,i}:P^{\prime}_{i+1}\to P^{\prime}_{i}
the corresponding adjacent map in the opposite direction.

We denote by
\alpha_{t,\tilde{t}}
the composition of maps
\alpha_{\tilde{t}-1,\tilde{t}}

\circ\dots

\circ

\alpha_{{t}+1,{t}+2}\circ

\alpha_{t,t+1}
and by
\alpha_{\tilde{t},t}
the composition
\alpha_{t+1,t}\circ\dots\circ\alpha_{\tilde{t}-1,\tilde{t}-2}\circ

\alpha_{\tilde{t},\tilde{t}-1}
.

For
t,t+1\in I_{0}
we denote by
\gamma_{t,t+1}:P_{t}\to P_{t+1}
and by
\gamma_{t+1,t}:P_{t+1}\to P_{t}
multiplication by
x
or
y
respectively. When
|I_{0}^{i}|=1
or
|I_{0}^{i+1}|=1
, we have a map
\epsilon_{t}:P^{\prime}_{t}\to P^{\prime}_{t}
inherited from the self-map
xy
from
P_{t}
to itself.

In addition, we have another set of self-maps which are maps from

to itself which are not homotopic to zero.
For

,

We will show in the main theorem that when
d_{0}+c_{0}=0
, the sum of B.1 and B.2 is homotopic to zero. We have left the dual pair in the catalog for convenience in decomposing other maps.

. Additional self-maps not necessarily irreducible

, a complex with

a single projetive can exist anly if

.

.

As in the case of C.1, this is usually a composition of
\alpha_{t,t\pm 1}
and
\alpha_{t\pm 1,t}
which we include as a convenience, but it is actually irreducible and needed in the catalog when
j=2
. In order to consider the homotopy relations, it is useful to consider the following linear combination of B.1, B.2 and C.2:

If
q<\overline{v-t}
then
d_{0}=0
and if
q<\overline{t-u}
then
c_{0}=0
. The map is homotopic to zero only if
b_{0}+c_{0}=h_{0}
and then the homotopy is

T=(c_{0}(xy)^{q-(\overline{t-u})}y^{\overline{t-u}},d_{0}(xy)^{q-(\overline{v-t})}x^{\overline{v-t}}).

(C.3)
Case of double projective in
Q_{t}
:
j=\overline{v-u}\leq r
and
\overline{u-v}=r-j.

For
e_{0}\neq 0
, the map is non-zero if
0\leq q_{1}\leq p-1-\overline{u-v}-hr
and well-defined if
q_{1}\geq p-\overline{\tilde{t}-v}-hr
. For
f_{0}\neq 0
, it is non-zero if
0\leq q_{2}\leq p-1-\overline{u-v}-hr
and well-defined if
q_{2}\geq p-\overline{u-\tilde{t}}-hr
. These occur for the maximal values of
h

•

. When

, self-maps with double projectives.

(D.3)
This is a special case, occuring only when
r=2j<p
and
h
is maximal with
hr<p
. We combine two linearly independent maps. The map is well-defined for
e_{0}\neq 0
if
hr+\overline{v-t}\geq p
and well-defined for
f_{0}\neq 0
if
hr+\overline{t-u}\geq p
.

(E.3)

1
term zero, double projective. For
hr<p
and every

, such that

, or in the dual,
hr+\overline{v-t}\geq p
.

(E.5)
Maps of stalk complexes. Suppose the
I-I_{0}
contains more than a single connected component, that
r<p
and that
s=\overline{v_{i}-u_{i+1}}<p
. Maps from
P_{u_{i+1}}

P_{v_{i-1}}
given by
x^{s}
or
y^{s}
must be included in the catalog in order that we can obtain self maps of
P_{w}
given by
x^{r}
and
y^{r}
. These are similar to the maps
x^{j}
and
y^{j}
appearing in Diagram 3. If the interval in
I_{0}
containing
w
consists of a single index, then the self-map of
P_{w}
given by
xy
is irreducible.

4
Maps between components of an elementary tilting complex

In this section, we will prove the proposition justifying the listed maps given in the catalog. In
\lx@sectionsign\ref{catalog of intra-arc maps}
the adjacent maps in
A
are irreducible, as are the self-maps in
B
. Of the maps in
C
, the maps with the smallest powers of
xy
are sometimes irreducible. When
u+1=v-1=t
, the map
C.1
with
q=1
is irreducible.

Proposition 4.1
.

We are given a group algebra
F[C_{p}\times C_{p}))\rtimes C_{r}]
and an elementary equivalence determined by a subset
I_{0}
of the set of residues
I
.
For each interval
J_{i}
in
I-I_{0}
, the set of maps in the catalog, whose nature depends on the relative sizes of
r
and
p
and the location of
I_{0}
, are all well-defined and not homotopic to zero. All the maps internal to the set
I_{0}^{i}\cup J_{i}\cup I_{0}^{i+1}
can be written a linear combinations of compositions of maps in the catalog.

Proof.

1.

The maps in the catalog are well-defined and not homotopic to zero

We frequently use
x^{p}=y^{p}=0
as we mentioned before.
All the maps between two stalk complexes are well-defined by the definition of the Brauer correspondent. Also, all the maps with zero at one end of the main diagonal are well-defined.

A. Diagram 1

We want to prove that all maps in Diagram 1 are well-defined and to check homotopy to zero. Maps between stalk complexes are already well-defined. Now we will check all maps between stalk complexes and
2
-complexes as follows:

The long arrow.

and they are not homotopic to zero because one of the off-diagonal modules is the zero module.

and dually

. Maps that have a square containing a vertical identity can never be homotopic to zero.

the chain maps are well-defined because
x^{\overline{t-u}}\circ xy=y\circ x^{\overline{t-u+1}}
and dually

(xy)\cdot y^{\overline{v-t-1}}=y^{\overline{v-t}}\cdot x
.
We want to prove that these maps are not homotopic to zero. If we can find
T:P_{\overline{t+1}}\rightarrow P_{u}
, then since the horizontal map
x^{\overline{t-u}}
has a non-zero power of
x
, the composition with
T
does not give
y
, and the case on the right is dual.

A. Diagram 2
. Now we will look at Diagram
2
and consider maps that do not occur in Diagram
1
. We start with the long arrows in Diagram 2.

They are well-defined because
x^{p-1}\circ xy=0
and dually
y^{p-1}\circ xy=0
. Maps that have a zero module at one end of the off-diagonal can never be homotopic to zero.

80.79865pt\raise-27.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\overline{u+j-p}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

In this case
j>p
. The chain maps are well-defined because
(xy)\cdot y^{j-p}=xy^{j-p+1}
and dually
(xy)\cdot x^{j-p}=yx^{j-p+1}
. If
j>p
the maps are not homotopic to zero because if
T_{1}=x^{j-p+1}
then
x^{j-p+1}y^{j-p}\not=x
and dually, if
T_{2}=y^{j-p+1}
, then
y^{j-p+1}x^{j-p}\not=y
.

Again
j>p
. The chain maps are well-defined because
x^{\overline{j-p}}\cdot x=x^{j-p+1}
and dually
y^{j-p}\cdot y=y^{j-p+1}
. Maps that have a square containing a vertical identity map can never be homotopic to zero.

Let
j=\overline{v-u}
and
t\in J
.

They are well-defined because
\begin{pmatrix}id&0\\
0&xy\end{pmatrix}

\cdot

\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u+1}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t-1}}\end{pmatrix}

=

\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}

\cdot

x
as well as the dual case. They are not homotopic to zero because if in the map on the left, the first component of the homotopy is nonzero, the composition with the horizontal map contains
x^{2}
and if the second component is non-zero, the composition has a power of
y
. The other map is dual.

A. Diagram 3
. These maps occur in Diagram
3
when
j\leq p-1
or in Diagram
2
when
j=p
.

The chain maps are well-defined because
(xy)\cdot x^{j-1}-x^{j}\cdot y=0
and dually
-y^{j}\cdot x+xy\cdot y^{j-1}=0
. Maps which have a zero module at one end of the off-diagonal can never be homotopic to zero.

Self-maps B
.
We now check well-defined for the maps in
B
.

t\in J

For the first map, let
a\in P_{u}
and
b\in P_{v}
. We check the commutativity of the square by diagram chasing.

\displaystyle(a,b)\cdot{\begin{pmatrix}-b_{0}(xy)^{\overline{v-t}}&b_{0}x^{\overline{v-u}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\cdot{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}=-ab_{0}x^{\overline{t-u}}(xy)^{\overline{v-t}}+ab_{0}x^{\overline{v-u}}y^{\overline{v-t}}=0

For the second map

\displaystyle(a,b)\cdot{\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
c_{0}y^{\overline{v-u}}&-c_{0}(xy)^{\overline{t-u}}\end{pmatrix}}\cdot{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}=bc_{0}x^{\overline{t-u}}y^{\overline{v-u}}-bc_{0}(xy)^{\overline{t-u}}y^{\overline{v-t}}=0

Now we will prove that B.1 is not homotopic to zero, and we will prove while dealing with the maps of C that B.2 is homotopic to B.1.

For
t\in J
,

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 64.18529pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-64.18529pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}-d_{0}(xy)^{\overline{v-t}}&d_{0}x^{\overline{v-u}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.12415pt\raise 15.0789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.46358pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.25555pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{0}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.46358pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.52841pt\raise-11.64339pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{T}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.61192pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.12415pt\raise-52.41222pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

We need to find
T:P_{t}\rightarrow P_{u}\oplus P_{v}
and to check if

\displaystyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}\circ T

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle T\circ{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}

\displaystyle={\begin{pmatrix}-d_{0}(xy)^{\overline{v-t}}&d_{0}x^{\overline{v-u}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},}

Let us take as our candidate for homotopy the map
T:P_{t}\rightarrow P_{u}\oplus P_{v}
given by

{\begin{pmatrix}e_{0}y^{\overline{t-u}}(xy)^{q_{1}}&f_{0}x^{\overline{v-t}}(xy)^{q_{2}}\end{pmatrix}}
. By Lemma
3.2
, if
r>p
, this is the only possibilty. From our calculations, it will be clear that even if
r<p
, multiplying the components of
T
by various power of
x^{r}
or
y^{r}
could not give the desired composition.

So, if we look at the bottom triangle
{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}\circ T\not=0
unless
e_{0}+f_{0}=0
and
q_{1}+\overline{t-u}=q_{2}+\overline{v-t}
. If
\overline{v-t}\geq\overline{t-u}
, we set
q_{2}=q
,
q_{1}=q+\overline{v+u-2t}
. If
\overline{t-u}\geq\overline{v-t}
, we set
q_{1}=q
,
q_{2}=q+\overline{2t-u-v}
. Now we check the second condition for the case
\overline{t-u}\geq\overline{v-t}
.

-d_{0}(xy)^{\overline{v-t}}=x^{\overline{t-u}}e_{0}y^{\overline{v-t}}(xy)^{q}

which can only hold if
e_{0}=-d_{0},\overline{t-u}=\overline{v-t}
and
q=0
. However, if all those conditions hold, then we must have
0=y^{\overline{v-t}}e_{0}y^{\overline{t-u}}(xy)^{0}
, which cannot be true because
\overline{t-u}+\overline{v-t}=j<p
. The case
\overline{v-t}\geq\overline{t-u}
is similar, using the second column of the matrix.

Self-maps C
.

Most of the additional self-maps
C.1
are compositions of adjacent maps and therefore well-defined. If
j=2
, the maps in
C.1
are irreducible but can be checked by inspection. In the case of a single projective, we have compositions of adjacent maps up and down or down and up, in the case of double projectives we have compositions of the maps in Definition
3.2
.

C.1: Case of single projective:
j>p
.
For
q<\overline{t-u}
,
w<\overline{v-t},t\in J
.

When
q\geq\overline{t-u}
, we get a homotopy to the map on the left given by

T=(xy)^{q-\overline{t-u}}y^{\overline{t-u}}
. When
w\geq\overline{v-t}
, we get a homotopy for the second map given by
T=(xy)^{w-\overline{v-t}}y^{\overline{v-t}}

C.2: Case of double projective:
j\leq 2(p-1)

We only have to check our claim that the map below is homotopic to zero if
h_{0}=d_{0}+c_{0}
and not all the parameters are zero. We assume

c_{0}\neq 0
, in which case
q\geq\overline{t-u}
or else
d_{0}\neq 0
, in which case
q\geq\overline{v-t}
.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 120.7126pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-120.7126pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}(h_{0}-d_{0})(xy)^{q}&d_{0}(xy)^{q-(\overline{v-t})}x^{\overline{v-u}}\\
c_{0}(xy)^{q-(\overline{t-u})}y^{\overline{v-u}}&(h_{0}-c_{0})(xy)^{q}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.12415pt\raise 15.0789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.46358pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h_{0}(xy)^{q}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.46358pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.12415pt\raise-22.25441pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

We define the homotopy map
T:P_{t}\to P_{u}\otimes P_{v}
by

(c_{0}y^{\overline{(}t-u)}(xy)^{q-\overline{t-u}},d_{0}x^{\overline{v-t}}(xy)^{q-\overline{v-t}})

Then the bottom triangle yields
(c_{0}+d_{0})(xy)^{q}
and the upper triangle yields the desired matrix, with
c_{0}
written for
h_{0}-d_{0}
and
d_{0}
written for
h_{0}-c_{0}
.

Self-maps D: When
r<p
, maps with double projective.

\textbf{(D.1)}\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.28705pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.28705pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{r}&0\\
0&x^{r}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.12415pt\raise 15.0789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.46358pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x^{r}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.46358pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.12415pt\raise-22.25441pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{{t}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

well-defined because

(a,b)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot x^{r}=ax^{\overline{t-u}}\cdot x^{r}+by^{\overline{v-t}}\cdot x^{r}\\

On the other hand

\displaystyle(a,b)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{r}&0\\
0&x^{r}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}=ax^{r}\cdot x^{\overline{t-u}}+bx^{r}\cdot y^{\overline{v-t}}.

The case of
(D.2)
is dual.

(D.3)
This is a special case, occuring only when
r=2j<p
and
h
is maximal with
hr<p
. The map is well-defined for
f_{0}\neq 0
if
hr+\overline{t-u}\geq p
and is well-defined for
e_{0}\neq 0
if
hr+\overline{v-t}\geq p
.

A homotopy for the bottom triangle would be
(f_{0}x^{hr-\overline{t-u}},e_{0}y^{hr-\overline{v-t}})
, but this would not give the proper matrix when composing the upper triangle.

. Chain maps with vertical term zero.

, it can exist just if

.

term zero, stalk comples to single projective.

In the first of these, the horizontal map exists if
j>p
and
0\leq\overline{t-u}\leq j-p
. The vertical map exists and is nonzero for every
w\in I_{0}^{i}
,
t\in J
, such that
\overline{t-w}\leq p
. The map is well-defined because the composition of horizontal and vertical maps contains
x
to the power
p
. Maps that have a zero module at one end of the off-diagonal can never be homotopic to zero. The second map is dual, where
w
lies in
I_{0}^{i+1}.

(E.3)

Chain maps with degree
1
term zero, double projective. Well-defined for
hr<p
and every
t\in J
, such that
hr+\overline{\tilde{t}-u}\geq p
, or in the dual,
hr+\overline{v-\tilde{t}}\geq p
.

The first chain map is well-defined if
hr+\overline{t-u}\geq p
. Dually, the second chain map is
well-defined if
hr+\overline{v-t}\geq p
. Maps that have a zero module at one end of the off-diagonal can never be homotopic to zero.

(E.5)
Maps between stalk complexes allowed by Lemma
3.2
must be well-defined and of course cannot be homotopic to zero, since there is no square.

2.

All maps internal to
I_{0}^{i}\cup J_{i}\cup I_{0}^{i+1}
are linear combinations of compositions of the maps in the catalog

In this section of the proof, we will consider pairs
P^{\prime}_{t}
and
P^{\prime}_{\tilde{t}}
of indecomposable components of the tilting complex of the elementary equivalence determined by the subinterval
I_{0}
. The irreducible components are of two kinds, stalk complexes when
t\in I_{0}
and complexes of length two when
t\in I-I_{0}
. We will first determine the general well-defined chain map, not necessarily homogeneous, between this pair of complexes, having constant recourse to Lemma
3.2
, which gives the possible maps between the indecomposable projectives
P_{i}
. We then attempt to write the general map as a linear combination of non-zero, well-defined homogeneous chain maps. Finally, we will decompose each homogeneous chain map as a composition of homogeneous chain maps from the catalog. In Case 2.1, we will deal with all maps between stalk complexes, in Cases 2.2, 2.3, and 2.4 we will deal with maps between two-term complexes, and in Case 2.5, with maps between stalk complexes and two-term complexes. We establish a general notation for maps between two-term complexes
P^{\prime}_{t}
and
P^{\prime}_{\tilde{t}}
where
t,\tilde{t}\in J
,
t\preceq\tilde{t}
.

Suppose
t,\tilde{t}\in I_{0}^{i}
or
t,\tilde{t}\in I_{0}^{i+1}
. Each individual map
x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}(xy)^{q}
or

is a composition of maps

in the catalog, where

\gamma

or

.

Suppose that
t\in I_{0}^{i}
,
\tilde{t}\in I_{0}^{i+1}
, and

\overline{\tilde{t}-t}<p
, which implies
j<p
,
the general map given above is a linear combination with coefficients
a_{q}
or
b_{q}
of the homogeneous maps in the sums, each of which is a composition of the
\gamma_{k,k+1}
, sometimes including
\epsilon_{t}
, with the jump map from Diagram 3, given by
x^{j}
or
y^{j}
,
followed by
q
self-maps
xy
, each
of which is a composition of two maps
\gamma_{t,t+1}
and
\gamma_{t+1,t}
or a copy of
\epsilon_{t}
maps.

Case 2.2
: Single projective, where
Q_{t}
,
Q_{\tilde{t}}=P_{u}
. We see from the diagrams in the catalog that such
Q_{t},Q_{\tilde{t}}
occur only when
j>p
, where we have
u+1\preceq t\preceq\tilde{t}\preceq u+(j-p)
. By Lemma
3.2
, in a special case where
i=k
and under the assumption
j>p
, all the non-zero maps
l_{0},k_{0}
between
P_{u}\to P_{u}
have the form
\sum_{q=0}^{p-1}c_{q}(xy)^{q}
and
\sum_{w=0}^{p-1}d_{w}(xy)^{w}
respectively.

Now we try to simplify these sums by subtracting maps that are homotopic to zero. Considering the left-hand square, we are in the case given in Lemma
3.1
with
s=u
, and conclude that
\overline{t-u}+\overline{\tilde{t}-t}=\overline{\tilde{t}-u}
. If we have a homogeneous map in degree zero of
(xy)^{q}
, then the chain map is well-defined if the map in degree
1
is

x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}(xy)^{q}
.

When
q\geq\overline{t-u}
then
we then have a homotopy to zero

T:P_{t}\rightarrow P_{u}
given by
(y^{\overline{t-u}}(xy)^{q-\overline{t-u}})
. The composition of the upper horizontal map and
T
in the upper triangle gives
(xy)^{q}
as needed, while the composition of
T
with the lower horizontal maps, using Lemma
3.1
gives

(xy)^{q-\overline{t-u}}y^{\overline{t-u}}x^{\overline{t-u}+\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}
. Pulling out the maximal power of
xy
and moving it to the end using commutativity, we have the desired vertical map
x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}(xy)^{q}
.
Subtracting off these homotopic-to-zero chain maps, we can assume that
\ell_{0}=\sum_{q=0}^{\min(p-1,\overline{t-u})}c_{q}(xy)^{q}
.

For each
q
in this range, we subtract off
c_{q}
times the composition of maps from the catalog
\delta^{q}\circ\alpha_{t,\tilde{t}}
, and we get the situation that
\ell_{0}=0
. For such a map to be well-defined, we must have
0=x^{\overline{t-u}}\circ\ell_{1}
. These maps are compositions of the maps in E.1, composed with
\delta_{t}^{q}
. Similarly for the map
k_{1}
or
\delta_{\tilde{t}}^{w}
. The case of
Q_{t},Q_{\tilde{t}}=P_{v}
is dual.

Case 2.3
: Double projectives, where
Q_{t}=Q_{\tilde{t}}=P_{u}\oplus P_{v}
. Of all the five cases, this is the most difficult, since we may have
r<p
, in which case we get the full sums in Lemma
3.2
.

Looking at the powers of
x
that occur in the horizontal maps in Diagram 2, where
p\leq j\leq 2(p-1)
and recalling that
j=\overline{v-u}
, then
\overline{(j-p)+1}\leq\overline{t-u}\leq\overline{\tilde{t}-u}\leq\overline{p-1}
. According to Diagram 3, where
2\leq j\leq p-1
then
1\leq\overline{t-u}\leq\overline{\tilde{t}-u}\leq\overline{j-1}
.

We recall that
u\in I_{0}^{i}
,
v\in I_{0}^{i+1}
. Assume that
t\preceq\tilde{t}\in J
. Let
a\in P_{u}
and
b\in P_{v}
. From our calculation of maps among projectives in Lemma
3.2
, we have that all maps
P^{\prime}_{t}\to P^{\prime}_{\tilde{t}}
are of the following form

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 76.5812pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-76.5812pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}(x,y)&a_{12}(x,y)\\
a_{21}(x,y)&a_{22}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.75783pt\raise 15.0789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.06601pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.06601pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.73094pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell_{1}(x,y)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.73094pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.75783pt\raise-22.25441pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\tilde{t}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

where, by Lemma
3.2
,

a_{ii}(x,y)=\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q_{ii}=0}^{(p-1-hr)}a_{q_{ii}}^{iih}\cdot x^{hr}(xy)^{q_{ii}}

+\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}_{ii}=0}^{(p-1-hr)}b_{q^{\prime}_{ii}}^{iih}\cdot y^{hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}_{ii}}

a_{12}(x,y)=1_{\overline{v-u}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{v-u}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q_{12}=0}^{p-1-(\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr)}a_{q_{12}}^{12h}\cdot x^{\overline{v-u}+hr}(xy)^{q_{12}}

+1_{\overline{u-v}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}_{12}=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{v-u}+hr))}b_{q}^{12h}\cdot y^{\overline{u-v}+hr}(xy)^{q_{21}}

a_{21}(x,y)=1_{\overline{u-v}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{u-v}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q_{21}=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{u-v}+hr))}a_{q}^{21h}\cdot x^{\overline{u-v}+hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}_{21}}

+1_{\overline{v-u}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{u-v}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q_{21}=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{u-v}+hr))}b_{q}^{21h}\cdot y^{\overline{}+hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}_{21}}

and, finally,

\ell_{1}(x,y)=1_{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr))}c_{q}^{h}\cdot x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr}(xy)^{q}

+1_{\overline{t-\tilde{t}}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}^{(p-1-(\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr))}d_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\cdot y^{\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}}

We now do diagram chasing on our diagram.
When calculating the diagonal map using the upper triangle, we get

(a,b)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\ell_{1}(x,y)=(ax^{\overline{t-u}}+by^{\overline{v-t}})\cdot\ell_{1}(x,y)

When we use the lower triangle, we have

(a,b)\cdot{\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}(x,y)&a_{12}(x,y)\\
a_{21}(x,y)&a_{22}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=(aa_{11}(x,y)+ba_{21}(x,y),aa_{12}(x,y)+ba_{22}(x,y))\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}}\end{pmatrix}

The coefficients of
a
and the coefficients of
b
must match in these expressions, giving us two equations

\displaystyle(a_{11}(x,y)x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}}+a_{12}(x,y)y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}})

\displaystyle=x^{\overline{t-u}}\cdot\ell_{1}(x,y)

(4.1)

\displaystyle(a_{21}(x,y)x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}}+a_{22}(x,y)y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}})

\displaystyle=y^{\overline{v-{t}}}\cdot\ell_{1}(x,y)

(4.2)

We want to show, under the assumption that this general map is well-defined, that the map is a linear combination of compositions of maps from the catalog. We first try to diagonalize the matrix, using maps from the catalog whose right vertical map is zero.
The first sum in
a_{12}
is eliminated by subtracting copies of B.1 composed with D.1 and C.2.
When these have been removed, we are left with

1_{\overline{u-v}<p}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}_{12}=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{v-u}+hr))}b_{q}^{12h}\cdot y^{\overline{u-v}+hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}_{21}}

The case of the double projective occurs only when
j=\overline{v-u}\leq 2(p-1)
and for this second sum to be non-zero we need
r-j=\overline{u-v}<p
. Furthermore, if
r>p
, then the upper limit of the first summation is zero, so we must have
h=0
.

Whenever
q^{\prime}_{12}\geq\overline{v-u}
we can remove
compositions with B.1, and we will assume that this has been done. We now use the map in C.2 with parameters
b_{0},c_{0},h_{0}
, where we assume
c_{0}=0
and
h_{0}=b_{0}
. Since this means that
h_{0}=c_{0}+b_{0}
, the map is homotopic to zero, and thus its compositions with

\alpha_{t,\tilde{t}}
and with powers of D.2 are also homotopic to zero.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 83.95012pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-83.95012pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}0&h_{0}(xy)^{q-(\overline{v-t})}x^{\overline{v-u}}y^{hr}\\
0&h_{0}(xy)^{q}y^{hr}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.12415pt\raise 15.0789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.46358pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h_{0}(xy)^{q}y^{hr}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.46358pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.12415pt\raise-22.25441pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Using Lemma
3.1
,
\overline{u-v}+\overline{v-u}=r
and
\overline{v-u}=\overline{v-t}+\overline{t-u}
, giving us that the upper right-hand entry in the matrix becomes
h_{0}(xy)^{q+\overline{t-u}}y^{\overline{v-u}+(h-1)r}
. Composing with
\alpha_{t,\tilde{t}}
will only multiply by a power of
xy
. Thus for proper choice of
q^{\prime}_{12}
, we can use this map to remove the remaining terms of
a_{12}(x,y)
. A dual procedure for eliminating
a_{21}(x,y)
will successfully diagonalize the matrix.

Once the matrix is diagonal, equations 4.1 and 4.2 assume a simple form that allows us to solve immediately for
a_{11}
and
a_{22}
whenever the map is well-defined. We separate
\ell_{1}
into the two possible sums and treat each separately. We start with the first sum, assuming
\overline{\tilde{t}-t}<p
and solve

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 76.5812pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-76.5812pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}(x,y)&0\\
0&a_{22}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.75783pt\raise 15.0789pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{t-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-t}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.06601pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.06601pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.73094pt\raise-18.66666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.8767pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr))}c_{q}^{h}\cdot x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr}(xy)^{q}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.73094pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{u}\oplus P_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.75783pt\raise-22.25441pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}}\\
y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}}\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 70.79865pt\raise-37.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\tilde{t}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Using 4.1 and the fact that
\overline{\tilde{t}-u}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+\overline{t-u}
, we get

a_{11}(x,y)=\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr))}c_{q}^{h}\cdot x^{hr}(xy)^{q}

Using 4.2 and the fact that
\overline{v-t}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+\overline{v-\tilde{t}}

we get

a_{22}(x,y)=\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q=0}^{(p-1-(\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+hr))}c_{q}^{h}\cdot x^{hr}(xy)^{\overline{\tilde{t}-t}+q}

The final map obtained from the first sum is a linear combination for various values of
h
of C.2 composed with D.1
h
times, followed by a composition with
\alpha_{t,\tilde{t}}
.

Now, assuming that
\overline{v-t}<p
, we must do something similar with
the second sum. Instead of writing the chain map again as in the case of the first sum, we will go directly to equations 4.1 and 4.2, using the second sum in
\ell_{1}

\displaystyle(a_{11}(x,y)x^{\overline{\tilde{t}-u}})

\displaystyle=x^{\overline{t-u}}\cdot\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}^{(p-1-(\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr))}d_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\cdot y^{\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}}

(4.3)

\displaystyle(a_{22}(x,y)y^{\overline{v-\tilde{t}}})

\displaystyle=y^{\overline{v-{t}}}\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}^{(p-1-(\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr))}d_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\cdot y^{\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}}

(4.4)

Since we cannot simply cancel the power of
x
in equation (4.3), we must make up the missing power of
x
from the powers of
xy
, which produces an extra copy of
y^{r}
. The final values are

\displaystyle a_{11}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}^{(p-1-(\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr))}d_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\cdot y^{(h+1)r}(xy)^{q^{\prime}-\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}

(4.5)

\displaystyle a_{22}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=0}^{\lfloor\frac{p-1-\overline{t-\tilde{t}}}{r}\rfloor}\sum_{q^{\prime}=\overline{\tilde{t}-t}}^{(p-1-(\overline{t-\tilde{t}}+hr))}d_{q^{\prime}}^{h}\cdot y^{hr}(xy)^{q^{\prime}}

(4.6)

The final map obtained from the first sum is a linear combination for various values of
h
of C.2 composed with D.2
h
times, followed by a composition with
\alpha_{t,\tilde{t}}
.

Case 2.4
: In this case we want to combine single and double projectives. Assume first that
Q_{t}=P_{u}
,
Q_{\tilde{t}}=P_{u}\oplus P_{v}
. For the map
Q_{t}\rightarrow P_{t}
to be non-zero, we need
j>p
, as we see from Diagram 2. Since
r\geq j
, for all the maps in Lemma
3.2
we have
h=0
. Since
j=\overline{v-u}
, there are no direct maps from
P_{u}
to
P_{v}
, so
the most general map is

\begin{array}[]{cc}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 42.30449pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.51854pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}(x,y)&0\end{pmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 32.3481pt\raise 7.3478pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-4.3478pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x^{\overline{t-u}}}$}}}\kern
